Yeah… I do. I feel pretty lousy afterward. Ashamed, mostly. Kind of disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.

Yeah… I’ve tried. Plenty of times. I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Roy, just shut the bag, put it down, go back inside… and I mean, I know what I’m supposed to do.

But it’s like once I’ve started, the switch is already flipped. My hands just keep going back for more even while I’m arguing with myself in my head. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m not usually someone who—well, at work if something needs doing, I do it, you know? But with that, in the moment, I don’t feel like I can actually stop until I’ve gone way past what I meant to eat.

Yeah, it’s usually not like an all-day grazing thing, if that’s what you mean. It’s more packed into a short stretch.

Like after a late shift, I’ll swing through for takeout — and I tell myself it’s dinner, you know, because I was working late — but then I’ll end up with two burgers, fries, a shake… and I’ll eat that pretty quick. Then I get home and somehow I’m out in the garage going through leftovers like it’s a second supper.

I mean, I can spend all day at the plant doing the same routine, clock in, clock out, everything measured down to the minute, and then after work it’s like the time gets fuzzy. But yeah, the actual eating part, that’s usually over a couple hours, not spread out forever.

1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: For the past few months, there’s usually at least one night a week—and often two or three—where I end up doing this after a late shift or a rough day on the line.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I’m still getting to work and keeping up with church, but I’ve been more withdrawn at home because I’m spending energy covering up where I stopped and why I’m not hungry for dinner.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about five months, since I was put in the acting lead role on the CNC line.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S049\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: When you're eating during these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual, like you're rushing through it without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Especially if I’m in my truck or out in the workshop. I’ll eat so fast that half the time I don’t even really taste it.\n\nIt’s almost like I’m trying to get it down before somebody comes around or notices, even if there’s nobody actually there. Which is stupid, I know. The workshop’s mine, mostly, and my truck is just—well, it’s not like I’m on a lunch break at the plant with guys walking by. But I’ll still rush through it like that, just kind of shoveling it in without really thinking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, even if you didn’t mean to eat that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Not every—well, I guess enough that I notice it.\n\nBy the time I finally stop, my stomach’s so tight against my work pants that I’m sitting there leaned back, like I don’t even want to move for a while. It’s that kind of uncomfortable where you’re thinking, why did I keep going?\n\nAnd those pants aren’t exactly forgiving, either. I’ve worn the same kind for years at the plant, and they’re stiff as cardboard when they come out of the wash. But yeah… I end up way fuller than I meant to be.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these episodes where you feel out of control with eating, does it happen within a short period of time — like over the course of just a couple hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually not like an all-day grazing thing, if that’s what you mean. It’s more packed into a short stretch.\n\nLike after a late shift, I’ll swing through for takeout — and I tell myself it’s dinner, you know, because I was working late — but then I’ll end up with two burgers, fries, a shake… and I’ll eat that pretty quick. Then I get home and somehow I’m out in the garage going through leftovers like it’s a second supper.\n\nI mean, I can spend all day at the plant doing the same routine, clock in, clock out, everything measured down to the minute, and then after work it’s like the time gets fuzzy. But yeah, the actual eating part, that’s usually over a couple hours, not spread out forever.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like admitting that, but yeah.

Like I’ll be at dinner with my wife and act like I’m not that hungry, just kind of pick at what’s there, say I had a big lunch or whatever. And then later, if she’s busy watching something or on the phone with her sister — her sister can talk forever, I swear — I’ll slip out to the truck or out by the garage and eat there.

It’s not even comfortable, eating in the truck like that. Stuff balanced on the seat, napkins everywhere. But I’d rather do that than have her see how much I’m actually putting away. It’s embarrassing. She wouldn’t be cruel about it, but… I don’t want that look, you know?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Please have a seat. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Uh… yeah. I guess what brings me in is my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand, especially after late shifts. I’ll stop for takeout, and it’s not just a sandwich or something — it’ll be like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I get home and I’m still poking around, finishing leftovers out in the garage like it’s some second dinner.\n\nI don’t know, it sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so sometimes I tell myself I’m just hungry from the shift, but… it’s more than that.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating large amounts of food, especially after your shifts, do you feel like you can’t control it — like it’s hard to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. Once I start, it’s like a switch gets flipped or something. I’ll be standing there with the bag open, and in my head I’m saying, Roy, shut the bag and go inside, you don’t need any more of this.\n\nAnd then my hand just… keeps going back in. Like I’m watching myself do it.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I can follow procedures all day — safety stuff, timing, all that — but with that food in front of me, after a shift, it’s like the sensible part of me gets shoved to the side.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating, how do you feel about yourself? Do you feel guilty, ashamed, or upset with how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I feel pretty lousy afterward. Ashamed, mostly. Kind of disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s strange because I’ve always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined person — I mean, at work people count on me, I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do. My dad was like that too, big on being dependable, not making excuses… anyway.\n\nSo when I’m out there eating like that, or sneaking food like I don’t want anyone to see, afterward it just hits me like, what are you doing? It feels like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: After you eat like that, do you ever try to make up for it by making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do that, exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely think, okay, tomorrow you’ve got to get yourself together, maybe skip the junk, that kind of thing.\n\nBut as far as… making myself sick or taking something, I don’t really know—no, that’s not what usually happens. The exercise thing, I mean, I’m on my feet a lot at work already, and my knees aren’t exactly what they used to be, so it’s not like I go run five miles or anything. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about those times when you're eating — do you ever feel like you can't stop, even if you want to, or like you're not really in control of what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s… that’s exactly what it feels like. Like once I start, something sort of flips. I’ll be standing there telling myself, Roy, close the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then my hand just keeps reaching back in anyway.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking, Oh great, let’s keep doing this. I’m usually already mad at myself while it’s happening. But it’s like the stopping part doesn’t kick in. I don’t know… kind of like when a machine at work keeps cycling even after you know something’s off—sorry, that’s probably a weird comparison. But yeah, I don’t feel fully in control of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to stop yourself from eating during one of these episodes, and if so, do you feel like you can’t actually stop until you’ve eaten a lot more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve tried. Plenty of times. I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Roy, just shut the bag, put it down, go back inside… and I mean, I know what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it’s like once I’ve started, the switch is already flipped. My hands just keep going back for more even while I’m arguing with myself in my head. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m not usually someone who—well, at work if something needs doing, I do it, you know? But with that, in the moment, I don’t feel like I can actually stop until I’ve gone way past what I meant to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating during these episodes, are you actually hungry, or does it feel like you’re eating even though you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that part, honestly. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I’m just getting a snack, but whether I’m actually hungry-hungry… I don’t always stop and check.\n\nIt’s funny, at work I can tell you exactly when I need lunch because my stomach’s growling and I’m watching the clock, but at home it’s more… I don’t know, automatic maybe? I’d have to think about it more. I’m not sure I could say for certain either way.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual, like you're rushing through it without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Especially if I’m in my truck or out in the workshop. I’ll eat so fast that half the time I don’t even really taste it.\n\nIt’s almost like I’m trying to get it down before somebody comes around or notices, even if there’s nobody actually there. Which is stupid, I know. The workshop’s mine, mostly, and my truck is just—well, it’s not like I’m on a lunch break at the plant with guys walking by. But I’ll still rush through it like that, just kind of shoveling it in without really thinking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, even if you didn’t mean to eat that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know… I mean, I notice it, sure. Hard not to when your clothes fit different or you catch yourself in the mirror getting ready for work.

But “really focused,” I’m not sure. I’m not standing there measuring myself or anything like that. Mostly I just get irritated, like when a shirt pulls weird or my belt’s on a different notch. Then I’ll think about it for a bit and go on with my day.

Although, those work uniforms, they make everybody look bad, honestly. Same gray pants, same stiff shirts — nobody’s winning a fashion contest at the plant. So I don’t know if it’s my body I’m thinking about or just feeling uncomfortable in general. I guess it bothers me some, but I’m not sure it’s the main thing.

Please have a seat. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Uh… yeah. I guess what brings me in is my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand, especially after late shifts. I’ll stop for takeout, and it’s not just a sandwich or something — it’ll be like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I get home and I’m still poking around, finishing leftovers out in the garage like it’s some second dinner.\n\nI don’t know, it sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so sometimes I tell myself I’m just hungry from the shift, but… it’s more than that.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating large amounts of food, especially after your shifts, do you feel like you can’t control it — like it’s hard to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. Once I start, it’s like a switch gets flipped or something. I’ll be standing there with the bag open, and in my head I’m saying, Roy, shut the bag and go inside, you don’t need any more of this.\n\nAnd then my hand just… keeps going back in. Like I’m watching myself do it.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I can follow procedures all day — safety stuff, timing, all that — but with that food in front of me, after a shift, it’s like the sensible part of me gets shoved to the side.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating, how do you feel about yourself? Do you feel guilty, ashamed, or upset with how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I feel pretty lousy afterward. Ashamed, mostly. Kind of disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s strange because I’ve always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined person — I mean, at work people count on me, I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do. My dad was like that too, big on being dependable, not making excuses… anyway.\n\nSo when I’m out there eating like that, or sneaking food like I don’t want anyone to see, afterward it just hits me like, what are you doing? It feels like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: After you eat like that, do you ever try to make up for it by making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do that, exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely think, okay, tomorrow you’ve got to get yourself together, maybe skip the junk, that kind of thing.\n\nBut as far as… making myself sick or taking something, I don’t really know—no, that’s not what usually happens. The exercise thing, I mean, I’m on my feet a lot at work already, and my knees aren’t exactly what they used to be, so it’s not like I go run five miles or anything. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about those times when you're eating — do you ever feel like you can't stop, even if you want to, or like you're not really in control of what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s… that’s exactly what it feels like. Like once I start, something sort of flips. I’ll be standing there telling myself, Roy, close the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then my hand just keeps reaching back in anyway.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking, Oh great, let’s keep doing this. I’m usually already mad at myself while it’s happening. But it’s like the stopping part doesn’t kick in. I don’t know… kind of like when a machine at work keeps cycling even after you know something’s off—sorry, that’s probably a weird comparison. But yeah, I don’t feel fully in control of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to stop yourself from eating during one of these episodes, and if so, do you feel like you can’t actually stop until you’ve eaten a lot more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve tried. Plenty of times. I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Roy, just shut the bag, put it down, go back inside… and I mean, I know what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it’s like once I’ve started, the switch is already flipped. My hands just keep going back for more even while I’m arguing with myself in my head. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m not usually someone who—well, at work if something needs doing, I do it, you know? But with that, in the moment, I don’t feel like I can actually stop until I’ve gone way past what I meant to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating during these episodes, are you actually hungry, or does it feel like you’re eating even though you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that part, honestly. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I’m just getting a snack, but whether I’m actually hungry-hungry… I don’t always stop and check.\n\nIt’s funny, at work I can tell you exactly when I need lunch because my stomach’s growling and I’m watching the clock, but at home it’s more… I don’t know, automatic maybe? I’d have to think about it more. I’m not sure I could say for certain either way.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual, like you're rushing through it without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Especially if I’m in my truck or out in the workshop. I’ll eat so fast that half the time I don’t even really taste it.\n\nIt’s almost like I’m trying to get it down before somebody comes around or notices, even if there’s nobody actually there. Which is stupid, I know. The workshop’s mine, mostly, and my truck is just—well, it’s not like I’m on a lunch break at the plant with guys walking by. But I’ll still rush through it like that, just kind of shoveling it in without really thinking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, even if you didn’t mean to eat that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Not every—well, I guess enough that I notice it.\n\nBy the time I finally stop, my stomach’s so tight against my work pants that I’m sitting there leaned back, like I don’t even want to move for a while. It’s that kind of uncomfortable where you’re thinking, why did I keep going?\n\nAnd those pants aren’t exactly forgiving, either. I’ve worn the same kind for years at the plant, and they’re stiff as cardboard when they come out of the wash. But yeah… I end up way fuller than I meant to be.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these episodes where you feel out of control with eating, does it happen within a short period of time — like over the course of just a couple hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually not like an all-day grazing thing, if that’s what you mean. It’s more packed into a short stretch.\n\nLike after a late shift, I’ll swing through for takeout — and I tell myself it’s dinner, you know, because I was working late — but then I’ll end up with two burgers, fries, a shake… and I’ll eat that pretty quick. Then I get home and somehow I’m out in the garage going through leftovers like it’s a second supper.\n\nI mean, I can spend all day at the plant doing the same routine, clock in, clock out, everything measured down to the minute, and then after work it’s like the time gets fuzzy. I’m not sure I could say for certain either way.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual, like you're rushing through it without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Especially if I’m in my truck or out in the workshop. I’ll eat so fast that half the time I don’t even really taste it.\n\nIt’s almost like I’m trying to get it down before somebody comes around or notices, even if there’s nobody actually there. Which is stupid, I know. The workshop’s mine, mostly, and my truck is just—well, it’s not like I’m on a lunch break at the plant with guys walking by. But I’ll still rush through it like that, just kind of shoveling it in without really thinking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, even if you didn’t mean to eat that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Not every—well, I guess enough that I notice it.\n\nBy the time I finally stop, my stomach’s so tight against my work pants that I’m sitting there leaned back, like I don’t even want to move for a while. It’s that kind of uncomfortable where you’re thinking, why did I keep going?\n\nAnd those pants aren’t exactly forgiving, either. I’ve worn the same kind for years at the plant, and they’re stiff as cardboard when they come out of the wash. But yeah… I end up way fuller than I meant to be.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these episodes where you feel out of control with eating, does it happen within a short period of time — like over the course of just a couple hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually not like an all-day grazing thing, if that’s what you mean. It’s more packed into a short stretch.\n\nLike after a late shift, I’ll swing through for takeout — and I tell myself it’s dinner, you know, because I was working late — but then I’ll end up with two burgers, fries, a shake… and I’ll eat that pretty quick. Then I get home and somehow I’m out in the garage going through leftovers like it’s a second supper.\n\nI mean, I can spend all day at the plant doing the same routine, clock in, clock out, everything measured down to the minute, and then after work it’s like the time gets fuzzy. But yeah, the actual eating part, that’s usually over a couple hours, not spread out forever.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like admitting that, but yeah.\n\nLike I’ll be at dinner with my wife and act like I’m not that hungry, just kind of pick at what’s there, say I had a big lunch or whatever. And then later, if she’s busy watching something or on the phone with her sister — her sister can talk forever, I swear — I’ll slip out to the truck or out by the garage and eat there.\n\nIt’s not even comfortable, eating in the truck like that. Stuff balanced on the seat, napkins everywhere. But I’d rather do that than have her see how much I’m actually putting away. It’s embarrassing. She wouldn’t be cruel about it, but… I don’t want that look, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself really focused on your weight or the way your body looks, maybe to the point where it bothers you a lot or affects how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I notice it, sure. Hard not to when your clothes fit different or you catch yourself in the mirror getting ready for work.\n\nBut “really focused,” I’m not sure. I’m not standing there measuring myself or anything like that. Mostly I just get irritated, like when a shirt pulls weird or my belt’s on a different notch. Then I’ll think about it for a bit and go on with my day.\n\nAlthough, those work uniforms, they make everybody look bad, honestly. Same gray pants, same stiff shirts — nobody’s winning a fashion contest at the plant. So I don’t know if it’s my body I’m thinking about or just feeling uncomfortable in general. I guess it bothers me some, but I’m not sure it’s the main thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… driving it, exactly.

I mean, sometimes I’ll feel lousy about how my stomach feels or how my pants fit, but when I’m in one of those eating spells, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I hate how I look, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more like I’m just in it, and then afterward I’ll think, well, that didn’t help anything.

But I don’t know. Maybe it’s mixed in there somewhere. Like the other day at work they changed the lockers around, and I had to squeeze past this row of guys during shift change — everybody’s got lunch pails and boots and all that — and I remember feeling awkward about taking up space. Then later that night I ate too much. But whether those are connected, I couldn’t really tell you.

Do you feel guilty, ashamed, or upset with how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I feel pretty lousy afterward. Ashamed, mostly. I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Roy, just shut the bag, put it down, go back inside… and I mean, I know what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it’s like once I’ve started, the switch is already flipped. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m not usually someone who—well, at work if something needs doing, I do it, you know? I’d have to think about it more. I’m not sure I could say for certain either way.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual, like you're rushing through it without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Especially if I’m in my truck or out in the workshop. I’ll eat so fast that half the time I don’t even really taste it.\n\nIt’s almost like I’m trying to get it down before somebody comes around or notices, even if there’s nobody actually there. Which is stupid, I know. The workshop’s mine, mostly, and my truck is just—well, it’s not like I’m on a lunch break at the plant with guys walking by. But I’ll still rush through it like that, just kind of shoveling it in without really thinking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, even if you didn’t mean to eat that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Not every—well, I guess enough that I notice it.\n\nBy the time I finally stop, my stomach’s so tight against my work pants that I’m sitting there leaned back, like I don’t even want to move for a while. It’s that kind of uncomfortable where you’re thinking, why did I keep going?\n\nAnd those pants aren’t exactly forgiving, either. I’ve worn the same kind for years at the plant, and they’re stiff as cardboard when they come out of the wash. But yeah… I end up way fuller than I meant to be.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these episodes where you feel out of control with eating, does it happen within a short period of time — like over the course of just a couple hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually not like an all-day grazing thing, if that’s what you mean. It’s more packed into a short stretch.\n\nLike after a late shift, I’ll swing through for takeout — and I tell myself it’s dinner, you know, because I was working late — but then I’ll end up with two burgers, fries, a shake… and I’ll eat that pretty quick. Then I get home and somehow I’m out in the garage going through leftovers like it’s a second supper.\n\nI mean, I can spend all day at the plant doing the same routine, clock in, clock out, everything measured down to the minute, and then after work it’s like the time gets fuzzy. But yeah, the actual eating part, that’s usually over a couple hours, not spread out forever.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Stuff balanced on the seat, napkins everywhere. But I’d rather do that than have her see how much I’m actually putting away. It’s embarrassing. Yeah… I think that’s closer to it, maybe. Not always like I’m crying or something, but there’s this urge that gets going.

Like once I start, it feels like somebody flipped a switch. I’ll even be saying in my head, “Roy, shut the bag and go back inside,” and then my hand’s already reaching in again. It’s stupid, because I know exactly what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping.

Stress probably makes it worse. Work’s been a mess with the line changes — they move one thing and suddenly everybody’s acting like the whole place is on fire — but even if it’s not some big dramatic upset, if I’m just off or wound up, yeah, it can hit me that way.

I mean, afterward I definitely think, okay, tomorrow you’ve got to get yourself together, maybe skip the junk, that kind of thing.\n\nBut as far as… making myself sick or taking something, I don’t really know—no, that’s not what usually happens. The exercise thing, I mean, I’m on my feet a lot at work already, and my knees aren’t exactly what they used to be, so it’s not like I go run five miles or anything. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about those times when you're eating — do you ever feel like you can't stop, even if you want to, or like you're not really in control of what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s… that’s exactly what it feels like. Like once I start, something sort of flips. I’ll be standing there telling myself, Roy, close the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then my hand just keeps reaching back in anyway.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking, Oh great, let’s keep doing this. I’m usually already mad at myself while it’s happening. But it’s like the stopping part doesn’t kick in. I don’t know… kind of like when a machine at work keeps cycling even after you know something’s off—sorry, that’s probably a weird comparison. But yeah, I don’t feel fully in control of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to stop yourself from eating during one of these episodes, and if so, do you feel like you can’t actually stop until you’ve eaten a lot more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve tried. Plenty of times. I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Roy, just shut the bag, put it down, go back inside… and I mean, I know what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it’s like once I’ve started, the switch is already flipped. My hands just keep going back for more even while I’m arguing with myself in my head. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m not usually someone who—well, at work if something needs doing, I do it, you know? But with that, in the moment, I don’t feel like I can actually stop until I’ve gone way past what I meant to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating during these episodes, are you actually hungry, or does it feel like you’re eating even though you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that part, honestly. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I’m just getting a snack, but whether I’m actually hungry-hungry… I don’t always stop and check.\n\nIt’s funny, at work I can tell you exactly when I need lunch because my stomach’s growling and I’m watching the clock, but at home it’s more… I don’t know, automatic maybe? I’d have to think about it more. I’m not sure I could say for certain either way.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual, like you're rushing through it without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Especially if I’m in my truck or out in the workshop. I’ll eat so fast that half the time I don’t even really taste it.\n\nIt’s almost like I’m trying to get it down before somebody comes around or notices, even if there’s nobody actually there. Which is stupid, I know. The workshop’s mine, mostly, and my truck is just—well, it’s not like I’m on a lunch break at the plant with guys walking by. But I’ll still rush through it like that, just kind of shoveling it in without really thinking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, even if you didn’t mean to eat that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Not every—well, I guess enough that I notice it.\n\nBy the time I finally stop, my stomach’s so tight against my work pants that I’m sitting there leaned back, like I don’t even want to move for a while. It’s that kind of uncomfortable where you’re thinking, why did I keep going?\n\nAnd those pants aren’t exactly forgiving, either. I’ve worn the same kind for years at the plant, and they’re stiff as cardboard when they come out of the wash. But yeah… I end up way fuller than I meant to be.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these episodes where you feel out of control with eating, does it happen within a short period of time — like over the course of just a couple hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually not like an all-day grazing thing, if that’s what you mean. It’s more packed into a short stretch.\n\nLike after a late shift, I’ll swing through for takeout — and I tell myself it’s dinner, you know, because I was working late — but then I’ll end up with two burgers, fries, a shake… and I’ll eat that pretty quick. Then I get home and somehow I’m out in the garage going through leftovers like it’s a second supper.\n\nI mean, I can spend all day at the plant doing the same routine, clock in, clock out, everything measured down to the minute, and then after work it’s like the time gets fuzzy. But yeah, the actual eating part, that’s usually over a couple hours, not spread out forever.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like admitting that, but yeah.\n\nLike I’ll be at dinner with my wife and act like I’m not that hungry, just kind of pick at what’s there, say I had a big lunch or whatever. And then later, if she’s busy watching something or on the phone with her sister — her sister can talk forever, I swear — I’ll slip out to the truck or out by the garage and eat there.\n\nIt’s not even comfortable, eating in the truck like that. Stuff balanced on the seat, napkins everywhere. But I’d rather do that than have her see how much I’m actually putting away. It’s embarrassing. She wouldn’t be cruel about it, but… I don’t want that look, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself really focused on your weight or the way your body looks, maybe to the point where it bothers you a lot or affects how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I notice it, sure. Hard not to when your clothes fit different or you catch yourself in the mirror getting ready for work.\n\nBut “really focused,” I’m not sure. I’m not standing there measuring myself or anything like that. Mostly I just get irritated, like when a shirt pulls weird or my belt’s on a different notch. Then I’ll think about it for a bit and go on with my day.\n\nAlthough, those work uniforms, they make everybody look bad, honestly. Same gray pants, same stiff shirts — nobody’s winning a fashion contest at the plant. So I don’t know if it’s my body I’m thinking about or just feeling uncomfortable in general. I guess it bothers me some, but I’m not sure it’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel upset or worried about your body shape or weight when you're having these episodes of eating, like it’s influencing why you eat the way you do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… driving it, exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll feel lousy about how my stomach feels or how my pants fit, but when I’m in one of those eating spells, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I hate how I look, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more like I’m just in it, and then afterward I’ll think, well, that didn’t help anything.\n\nBut I don’t know. Maybe it’s mixed in there somewhere. Like the other day at work they changed the lockers around, and I had to squeeze past this row of guys during shift change — everybody’s got lunch pails and boots and all that — and I remember feeling awkward about taking up space. Then later that night I ate too much. But whether those are connected, I couldn’t really tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong emotional urge or need to eat like this, especially when you're stressed, upset, or just feeling off?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s closer to it, maybe. Not always like I’m crying or something, but there’s this urge that gets going.\n\nLike once I start, it feels like somebody flipped a switch. I’ll even be saying in my head, “Roy, shut the bag and go back inside,” and then my hand’s already reaching in again. It’s stupid, because I know exactly what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping.\n\nStress probably makes it worse. Work’s been a mess with the line changes — they move one thing and suddenly everybody’s acting like the whole place is on fire — but even if it’s not some big dramatic upset, if I’m just off or wound up, yeah, it can hit me that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{
  "candidates": ["F50.2", "F50.8"],
  "note": "",
  "is_final": false
}

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself really focused on your weight or the way your body looks, maybe to the point where it bothers you a lot or affects how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I notice it, sure. Hard not to when your clothes fit different or you catch yourself in the mirror getting ready for work.\n\nBut “really focused,” I’m not sure. I’m not standing there measuring myself or anything like that. Mostly I just get irritated, like when a shirt pulls weird or my belt’s on a different notch. Then I’ll think about it for a bit and go on with my day.\n\nAlthough, those work uniforms, they make everybody look bad, honestly. Same gray pants, same stiff shirts — nobody’s winning a fashion contest at the plant. So I don’t know if it’s my body I’m thinking about or just feeling uncomfortable in general. I guess it bothers me some, but I’m not sure it’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel upset or worried about your body shape or weight when you're having these episodes of eating, like it’s influencing why you eat the way you do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… driving it, exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll feel lousy about how my stomach feels or how my pants fit, but when I’m in one of those eating spells, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I hate how I look, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more like I’m just in it, and then afterward I’ll think, well, that didn’t help anything.\n\nBut I don’t know. Maybe it’s mixed in there somewhere. Like the other day at work they changed the lockers around, and I had to squeeze past this row of guys during shift change — everybody’s got lunch pails and boots and all that — and I remember feeling awkward about taking up space. Then later that night I ate too much. But whether those are connected, I couldn’t really tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong emotional urge or need to eat like this, especially when you're stressed, upset, or just feeling off?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s closer to it, maybe. Not always like I’m crying or something, but there’s this urge that gets going.\n\nLike once I start, it feels like somebody flipped a switch. I’ll even be saying in my head, “Roy, shut the bag and go back inside,” and then my hand’s already reaching in again. It’s stupid, because I know exactly what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping.\n\nStress probably makes it worse. Work’s been a mess with the line changes — they move one thing and suddenly everybody’s acting like the whole place is on fire — but even if it’s not some big dramatic upset, if I’m just off or wound up, yeah, it can hit me that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel a lot of distress or emotional discomfort specifically about these episodes of eating, like they’re really bothering you or taking up a lot of your thoughts?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / production_worker / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\nmanifestation: \"Afterward I feel ashamed and kind of disgusted with myself, because I’ve always thought of myself as disciplined and dependable, and this makes me feel like I’m sneaking around and letting myself down.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… afterward, definitely. That’s usually when it hits me.

I’ll be standing there with the wrappers or whatever, and I just feel… ashamed, I guess. Kind of disgusted with myself. Which sounds harsh, but that’s the word that comes up.

I’ve always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined guy, you know? I show up to work, do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. My dad was big on that kind of thing too — if you say you’re going to do something, you do it. So when I’m sneaking food or eating like that when nobody’s around, it feels like I’m being some other version of myself. Like I’m letting myself down.

I don’t know if I sit and think about it all day, but it bothers me. More than I’d like to admit.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.2, F50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome. Please have a seat. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Uh… yeah. I guess what brings me in is my eating’s gotten kind of out of hand, especially after late shifts. I’ll stop for takeout, and it’s not just a sandwich or something — it’ll be like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I get home and I’m still poking around, finishing leftovers out in the garage like it’s some second dinner.\n\nI don’t know, it sounds ridiculous saying it out loud. I work production, so sometimes I tell myself I’m just hungry from the shift, but… it’s more than that.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating large amounts of food, especially after your shifts, do you feel like you can’t control it — like it’s hard to stop eating once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. Once I start, it’s like a switch gets flipped or something. I’ll be standing there with the bag open, and in my head I’m saying, Roy, shut the bag and go inside, you don’t need any more of this.\n\nAnd then my hand just… keeps going back in. Like I’m watching myself do it.\n\nIt’s weird, because at work I can follow procedures all day — safety stuff, timing, all that — but with that food in front of me, after a shift, it’s like the sensible part of me gets shoved to the side.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of eating, how do you feel about yourself? Do you feel guilty, ashamed, or upset with how much you ate?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I feel pretty lousy afterward. Ashamed, mostly. Kind of disgusted with myself, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s strange because I’ve always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined person — I mean, at work people count on me, I show up, I do what I’m supposed to do. My dad was like that too, big on being dependable, not making excuses… anyway.\n\nSo when I’m out there eating like that, or sneaking food like I don’t want anyone to see, afterward it just hits me like, what are you doing? It feels like I’m letting myself down.\n\nDoctor: After you eat like that, do you ever try to make up for it by making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising a lot more than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I do that, exactly. I mean, afterward I definitely think, okay, tomorrow you’ve got to get yourself together, maybe skip the junk, that kind of thing.\n\nBut as far as… making myself sick or taking something, I don’t really know—no, that’s not what usually happens. The exercise thing, I mean, I’m on my feet a lot at work already, and my knees aren’t exactly what they used to be, so it’s not like I go run five miles or anything. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about those times when you're eating — do you ever feel like you can't stop, even if you want to, or like you're not really in control of what or how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that’s… that’s exactly what it feels like. Like once I start, something sort of flips. I’ll be standing there telling myself, Roy, close the bag, go inside, you’re done, and then my hand just keeps reaching back in anyway.\n\nIt’s not like I’m thinking, Oh great, let’s keep doing this. I’m usually already mad at myself while it’s happening. But it’s like the stopping part doesn’t kick in. I don’t know… kind of like when a machine at work keeps cycling even after you know something’s off—sorry, that’s probably a weird comparison. But yeah, I don’t feel fully in control of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever tried to stop yourself from eating during one of these episodes, and if so, do you feel like you can’t actually stop until you’ve eaten a lot more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’ve tried. Plenty of times. I’ll be right in the middle of it thinking, Roy, just shut the bag, put it down, go back inside… and I mean, I know what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut it’s like once I’ve started, the switch is already flipped. My hands just keep going back for more even while I’m arguing with myself in my head. It’s embarrassing to say, because I’m not usually someone who—well, at work if something needs doing, I do it, you know? But with that, in the moment, I don’t feel like I can actually stop until I’ve gone way past what I meant to eat.\n\nDoctor: When you’re eating during these episodes, are you actually hungry, or does it feel like you’re eating even though you’re not really hungry?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’ve really paid attention to that part, honestly. I mean, sometimes I tell myself I’m just getting a snack, but whether I’m actually hungry-hungry… I don’t always stop and check.\n\nIt’s funny, at work I can tell you exactly when I need lunch because my stomach’s growling and I’m watching the clock, but at home it’s more… I don’t know, automatic maybe? I’d have to think about it more. I’m not sure I could say for certain either way.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual, like you're rushing through it without really thinking about it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I do. Especially if I’m in my truck or out in the workshop. I’ll eat so fast that half the time I don’t even really taste it.\n\nIt’s almost like I’m trying to get it down before somebody comes around or notices, even if there’s nobody actually there. Which is stupid, I know. The workshop’s mine, mostly, and my truck is just—well, it’s not like I’m on a lunch break at the plant with guys walking by. But I’ll still rush through it like that, just kind of shoveling it in without really thinking.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you find yourself eating until you feel uncomfortably full, even if you didn’t mean to eat that much?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that happens. Not every—well, I guess enough that I notice it.\n\nBy the time I finally stop, my stomach’s so tight against my work pants that I’m sitting there leaned back, like I don’t even want to move for a while. It’s that kind of uncomfortable where you’re thinking, why did I keep going?\n\nAnd those pants aren’t exactly forgiving, either. I’ve worn the same kind for years at the plant, and they’re stiff as cardboard when they come out of the wash. But yeah… I end up way fuller than I meant to be.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these episodes where you feel out of control with eating, does it happen within a short period of time — like over the course of just a couple hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s usually not like an all-day grazing thing, if that’s what you mean. It’s more packed into a short stretch.\n\nLike after a late shift, I’ll swing through for takeout — and I tell myself it’s dinner, you know, because I was working late — but then I’ll end up with two burgers, fries, a shake… and I’ll eat that pretty quick. Then I get home and somehow I’m out in the garage going through leftovers like it’s a second supper.\n\nI mean, I can spend all day at the plant doing the same routine, clock in, clock out, everything measured down to the minute, and then after work it’s like the time gets fuzzy. But yeah, the actual eating part, that’s usually over a couple hours, not spread out forever.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat alone during these episodes because you’re embarrassed about how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I mean, I don’t really like admitting that, but yeah.\n\nLike I’ll be at dinner with my wife and act like I’m not that hungry, just kind of pick at what’s there, say I had a big lunch or whatever. And then later, if she’s busy watching something or on the phone with her sister — her sister can talk forever, I swear — I’ll slip out to the truck or out by the garage and eat there.\n\nIt’s not even comfortable, eating in the truck like that. Stuff balanced on the seat, napkins everywhere. But I’d rather do that than have her see how much I’m actually putting away. It’s embarrassing. She wouldn’t be cruel about it, but… I don’t want that look, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself really focused on your weight or the way your body looks, maybe to the point where it bothers you a lot or affects how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I notice it, sure. Hard not to when your clothes fit different or you catch yourself in the mirror getting ready for work.\n\nBut “really focused,” I’m not sure. I’m not standing there measuring myself or anything like that. Mostly I just get irritated, like when a shirt pulls weird or my belt’s on a different notch. Then I’ll think about it for a bit and go on with my day.\n\nAlthough, those work uniforms, they make everybody look bad, honestly. And then later, if she’s busy watching something or on the phone with her sister — her sister can talk forever, I swear — I’ll slip out to the truck or out by the garage and eat there.\n\nIt’s not even comfortable, eating in the truck like that. Stuff balanced on the seat, napkins everywhere. But I’d rather do that than have her see how much I’m actually putting away. It’s embarrassing. She wouldn’t be cruel about it, but… I don’t want that look, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself really focused on your weight or the way your body looks, maybe to the point where it bothers you a lot or affects how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, I notice it, sure. Hard not to when your clothes fit different or you catch yourself in the mirror getting ready for work.\n\nBut “really focused,” I’m not sure. I’m not standing there measuring myself or anything like that. Mostly I just get irritated, like when a shirt pulls weird or my belt’s on a different notch. Then I’ll think about it for a bit and go on with my day.\n\nAlthough, those work uniforms, they make everybody look bad, honestly. Same gray pants, same stiff shirts — nobody’s winning a fashion contest at the plant. So I don’t know if it’s my body I’m thinking about or just feeling uncomfortable in general. I guess it bothers me some, but I’m not sure it’s the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel upset or worried about your body shape or weight when you're having these episodes of eating, like it’s influencing why you eat the way you do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s like… driving it, exactly.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll feel lousy about how my stomach feels or how my pants fit, but when I’m in one of those eating spells, I don’t think I’m sitting there thinking, “I hate how I look, so I’m going to eat.” It’s more like I’m just in it, and then afterward I’ll think, well, that didn’t help anything.\n\nBut I don’t know. Maybe it’s mixed in there somewhere. Like the other day at work they changed the lockers around, and I had to squeeze past this row of guys during shift change — everybody’s got lunch pails and boots and all that — and I remember feeling awkward about taking up space. Then later that night I ate too much. But whether those are connected, I couldn’t really tell you.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel a strong emotional urge or need to eat like this, especially when you're stressed, upset, or just feeling off?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s closer to it, maybe. Not always like I’m crying or something, but there’s this urge that gets going.\n\nLike once I start, it feels like somebody flipped a switch. I’ll even be saying in my head, “Roy, shut the bag and go back inside,” and then my hand’s already reaching in again. It’s stupid, because I know exactly what I’m doing, but I’m not really stopping.\n\nStress probably makes it worse. Work’s been a mess with the line changes — they move one thing and suddenly everybody’s acting like the whole place is on fire — but even if it’s not some big dramatic upset, if I’m just off or wound up, yeah, it can hit me that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a lot of distress or emotional discomfort specifically about these episodes of eating, like they’re really bothering you or taking up a lot of your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yeah… afterward, definitely. That’s usually when it hits me.\n\nI’ll be standing there with the wrappers or whatever, and I just feel… ashamed, I guess. Kind of disgusted with myself. Which sounds harsh, but that’s the word that comes up.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as a pretty disciplined guy, you know? I show up to work, do what I’m supposed to do, people can count on me. My dad was big on that kind of thing too — if you say you’re going to do something, you do it. So when I’m sneaking food or eating like that when nobody’s around, it feels like I’m being some other version of myself. Like I’m letting myself down.\n\nI don’t know if I sit and think about it all day, but it bothers me. More than I’d like to admit."
